<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Approximating minimum directed feedback vertex set within any fixed constant factor is NP-hard, even on unweighted digraphs.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every fixed real constant $A\ge1$, it is NP-hard to approximate minimum directed feedback vertex set within factor $A$ on unweighted digraphs. More precisely, there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from an NP-hard promise problem to instances $(G,k)$ with $k\in\mathbb N_{>0}$. The polynomial and its constants may depend on $A$. The reduction has the following alternatives: $$\textnormal{YES: }\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)\le k, \qquad \textnormal{NO: }\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)>Ak.$$
  - Explanation: Excludes every fixed constant approximation factor for directed feedback vertex set through a deterministic promise-gap reduction.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-games)): For every constant $\theta\in(0,1/2)$, there are constant label sets $X,Y$ such that it is $\mathrm{NP}$-hard, by a deterministic polynomial-time reduction, to distinguish two-to-one games of the following kinds:* 1. *there is a labeling of value at least $1-\theta$;* 2. *every labeling has value at most $\theta$.* *Every constraint is a total map $X\to Y$ with exactly two preimages of each label in $Y$. The label sets may depend on $\theta$.
  - Explanation: Supplies the imperfect-completeness two-to-one game gap used as the hardness source.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-friedgut)): For every $b\ge0$ and $\alpha>0$, there is a finite integer $J_{\mathrm{unif}}(b,\alpha)$ with the following property. For every finite $S$ and every Boolean function $f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ with $I_{1/2}(f)\le b$, there is a Boolean function $\widetilde f$, depending on at most $J_{\mathrm{unif}}(b,\alpha)$ coordinates, for which $$\Pr_{R\sim\mu_{1/2}^{S}}[\,f(R)\ne\widetilde f(R)\,]\le\alpha.$$ The bound is independent of $\lvert S\rvert$.
  - Explanation: Bounds the coordinate support of a uniform-measure Boolean approximant independently of the underlying finite set.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-dyadic-junta)): Let $p=2^{-k}$, where $k\ge1$ is an integer. For every $b\ge0$ and $\alpha>0$, there is a finite integer $J(p,b,\alpha)$, independent of $\lvert S\rvert$, such that every Boolean function $f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ with $I_p(f)\le b$ has a Boolean junta approximant on at most $J(p,b,\alpha)$ coordinates with error at most $\alpha$ under $\mu_p^S$.
  - Explanation: Transfers the alphabet-independent junta guarantee to a fixed dyadic product bias.
- **C5** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-evaluation-invariant)): Every global game labeling $\ell$ gives a well-defined real value on the rank vertices by $$b_{\mathbf d}(x)\longmapsto x(\ell(\mathbf d)),$$ where $\ell(\mathbf d)$ is the tuple of labels supplied by $\ell$. Every rank arc strictly increases this value. Consequently, the rank graph has no self-loops, and $o_1,\ldots,o_N$ are distinct vertices with an arc $o_j\to o_{j'}$ whenever $j<j'$.
  - Explanation: Makes global-label evaluation consistent with prefix identifications and strictly increasing on rank arcs.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-completeness)): If the input game has a labeling satisfying a fraction at least $1-\theta$ of its constraints under $\nu$, then the constructed digraph has a feedback vertex set of cost at most $4$.
  - Explanation: A nearly satisfying game labeling gives a low-cost deletion set with an acyclic remainder.
- **C7** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-polynomial)): For each fixed $A$, the preceding construction produces a loopless digraph with positive rational vertex costs in deterministic polynomial time in the game input size. Its vertex costs are bounded above by a constant depending only on $A$.
  - Explanation: Enumerates finite rational test outcomes in polynomial time when the approximation factor is fixed.
- **C8** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-spreading)): For every integer $L\ge3$ and every $\eta>0$, there are an integer $N\ge L$ and a rational probability distribution on increasing maps $\psi\colon[L]\hookrightarrow[N]$ such that, for all $I,I'\in\binom{[L]}{3}$, $$\label{eq:rank-spreading-tv} \mathop{\mathrm{TV}}\bigl(\mathcal L(\psi(I)),\mathcal L(\psi(I'))\bigr)\le\eta.$$ Here $\mathcal L$ denotes the law of a random variable, and all the laws in Equation <a href="#eq:rank-spreading-tv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-spreading-tv">[eq:rank-spreading-tv]</a> use the same random map $\psi$.
  - Explanation: Obtains a rational distribution of increasing embeddings whose triple image laws are uniformly close.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-goodness)): For each fixed allowed tuple $\mathbf d$, $$\label{eq:goodness-probability} \Pr_{\psi}[\mathbf d\text{ is bad at }\psi] \le 3\eta L^3 2^{\lvert\Lambda_{\mathbf d}\rvert} \le\rho.$$ The same upper bound $\rho$ holds when $\mathbf d$ is sampled independently of $\psi$.
  - Explanation: Bounds badness for a fixed or independently sampled tuple; simultaneous goodness of every tuple is not asserted.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-monotonicity)): If $\mathbf d$ is good at $\psi$, then $g_{\mathbf d}\colon2^{\Lambda_{\mathbf d}}\to\{0,1\}$ is monotone: $$E\subseteq E'\quad\Longrightarrow\quad g_{\mathbf d}(E)\le g_{\mathbf d}(E').$$ Moreover, $$g_{\mathbf d}(\varnothing)=0, \qquad g_{\mathbf d}(\Lambda_{\mathbf d})=1.$$
  - Explanation: Goodness converts order comparisons into a monotone Boolean function with the stated boundary values.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-compatibility)): Let $\mathbf s w$ be an allowed extension of $\mathbf s$. For every $E\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf s}$ and every increasing triple $(a,c,h)$, $$\label{eq:prefix-lift-bits} G_{\mathbf s w}(E\times\Lambda_w;a,c,h) =G_{\mathbf s}(E;a,c,h).$$ In particular, $g_{\mathbf s w}(E\times\Lambda_w)=g_{\mathbf s}(E)$. If $\mathbf s w$ is good at $\psi$, then so is $\mathbf s$. The same conclusions hold for a prefix obtained by dropping several final slots.
  - Explanation: Prefix identifications preserve comparison bits and pass goodness from an extension to its prefixes.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-wildcard-forcing)): Consider a wildcard test vertex $v_{\mathrm{test}}$ from Definition <a href="#def:wildcard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:wildcard">8</a>, with tuple $\mathbf d$, embedding $\psi$, ordered cells $C_1,\ldots,C_k$, and star set $Z$. Suppose that $\mathbf d$ is good at $\psi$. For an integer $d$ with $0\le d\le k$, put $$E=\Bigl(\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{d}C_\ell\Bigr)\setminus Z.$$ If $$\label{eq:wildcard-forcing-criterion} g_{\mathbf d}(E)=0, \qquad g_{\mathbf d}(E\cup Z)=1,$$ then $v_{\mathrm{test}}\in F$.
  - Explanation: A pivotal star set across an ordered cut forces deletion of its wildcard test vertex.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-prefix-refinement)): Draw prefix data of length $T$ and an independent embedding $\psi$. Write $\mathbf s_j=(u_1,\ldots,u_j)$, and let $C^{(j)}_1,\ldots,C^{(j)}_{2^j}$ be the partition at depth $j$. For $j=0$, this is the single cell $\Lambda_{\varnothing}$. Suppose that $\mathbf s_T$ is good, and let $\tau_j$ be the transition index at depth $j$. Then the full transition cell is nonempty and, for every $0\le j\le T$, $$C^{(T)}_{\tau_T} \subseteq C^{(j)}_{\tau_j}\times X^{T-j}.$$ Let $\mathcal D_{j-1}$ denote the shared data consisting of the same embedding, the first $j-1$ prefix slots, and the deterministic length $j-1$. For each $1\le j\le T$, the membership bit of slot $j$ throughout the full transition cell is $$\mathbf 1\{\lambda_j\in P_j\} =f_{u_j,\mathcal D_{j-1}}(P_j) \qquad \text{for every } (\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_T)\in C^{(T)}_{\tau_T}. \label{eq:transition-membership}$$
  - Explanation: The full transition cell refines earlier transition cells and its membership bits equal the conditioned function outputs.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-prefix-lower-bound)): Under the common law of $(\mathcal D,e)$, for every $i\in[M]$, $$\mathbb Eh_u(p_i)\ge\frac{p_i}{4}. \label{eq:prefix-lower-bound}$$
  - Explanation: The shared law yields an acceptance lower bound at every prescribed bias without conditioning that law on the favorable event.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pivotal-inequalities)): For every function in Definition <a href="#def:pivotal-budget" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:pivotal-budget">22</a> and every $0<p<1$, $$I_p(f)\le B(f), \qquad \mathbb E_{R\sim\mu_p^S} f(R)\le pB(f).$$
  - Explanation: The same permutation pivotality budget bounds total influence and acceptance at every product bias.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-pivotal-budget)): Under the common law of $\mathcal D$ and an independent edge $e=(u,v)\sim\nu$, $$\mathbb E B_u\le D+1, \qquad \mathbb E B_v\le D+1. \label{eq:pivotal-budget}$$
  - Explanation: Single-label test deletion costs control the expected pivotality budgets under the common endpoint law.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-comparison-charge)): For every $i\in[M]$, $$\label{eq:coupled-comparison} \Pr_{\mathrm c,i}[\,f_u(R_u)=1,\ f_v(R_v)=0\,] \le 2\rho+\frac{M2^T D}{K}.$$
  - Explanation: Coupled output disagreements force comparison-test deletions and are charged against the deletion cost.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-list-decoding)): Suppose a game has value at most $\theta$. Assign to every game vertex $w$ a list $J_w\subseteq\Lambda_w$, possibly empty, of cardinality at most an integer $J\ge1$. Then $$\Pr_{e=(u,v)\sim\nu} [\,\pi_e(J_u)\cap J_v\ne\varnothing\,]\le\theta J^2.$$ The same conclusion holds conditionally on any data independent of the fresh edge, if all the lists are fixed by those data.
  - Explanation: Game soundness bounds projection overlap of bounded lists chosen before the fresh edge.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-product-comparison)): For every $i\in[M]$, under the common distribution of $(\mathcal D,e)$, $$\label{eq:product-comparison} \mathbb E\bigl[h_u(p_i)(1-h_v(q_i))\bigr]\le\frac{p_i}{16}.$$
  - Explanation: Junta approximation and disjoint projection fibers turn the coupled comparison estimate into a product-measure bound.
- **C20** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-weighted-soundness)): If the game has value at most $\theta$, every feedback vertex set of the constructed weighted digraph has cost strictly greater than $D$.
  - Explanation: Dyadic tails of the same pivotality variable contradict its expected budget in the game soundness case.
- **C21** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-round-and-clone)): Let $G$ be a loopless digraph on $S\ge1$ vertices with positive rational vertex costs $w_v\le C$. Set $c_v=\lceil Sw_v\rceil$. Replace every vertex $v$ by an independent set $\mathcal C_v$ of $c_v$ unweighted vertices, and replace every arc $u\to v$ by all arcs from $\mathcal C_u$ to $\mathcal C_v$. Call the resulting digraph $\widehat G$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(\widehat G) =\min\left\{\sum_{v\in F}c_v: F\text{ is a feedback vertex set of }G\right\}. \label{eq:clone-exact-optimum}$$ The number of vertices of $\widehat G$ is at most $CS^2+S$. For fixed $C$, this is a deterministic polynomial construction in the encoding size of $G$ and its costs. Moreover:* 1. *a feedback vertex set of $G$ of cost at most $a$ yields a feedback vertex set of $\widehat G$ of size at most $S(a+1)$;* 2. *if every feedback vertex set of $G$ has cost greater than $b$, then $\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(\widehat G)>Sb$.
  - Explanation: Upward rational rounding and independent clusters preserve the rounded deletion optimum and control the additive cost loss.
- **C22** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-feedback-arc)): For every fixed real $A\ge1$, approximating directed feedback arc set within factor $A$ is NP-hard on unweighted simple loopless digraphs. More precisely, there is a deterministic polynomial reduction producing such a digraph $H$ and a positive integer $k$ with the promise gap $$\begin{array}{ll} \text{YES:}&\operatorname{DFAS}(H)\le k,\\[2pt] \text{NO:}&\operatorname{DFAS}(H)>Ak. \end{array}$$
  - Explanation: Transfers the constant-factor promise gap to directed feedback arc set on unweighted simple loopless digraphs.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
        "exact_quote": "For every fixed real constant $A\\ge1$, it is NP-hard to approximate minimum directed feedback vertex set within factor $A$ on unweighted digraphs. More precisely, there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from an NP-hard promise problem to instances $(G,k)$ with $k\\in\\mathbb N_{>0}$. The polynomial and its constants may depend on $A$. The reduction has the following alternatives: $$\\textnormal{YES: }\\mathop{\\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)\\le k,\n  \\qquad\n  \\textnormal{NO: }\\mathop{\\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)>Ak.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-102.5"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
          "exact_quote": "For every fixed real constant $A\\ge1$, it is NP-hard to approximate minimum directed feedback vertex set within factor $A$ on unweighted digraphs. The reduction has the following alternatives: $$\\textnormal{YES: }\\mathop{\\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)\\le k,\n  \\qquad\n  \\textnormal{NO: }\\mathop{\\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)>Ak.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-102.5"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-games",
        "exact_quote": "For every constant $\\theta\\in(0,1/2)$, there are constant label sets $X,Y$ such that it is $\\mathrm{NP}$-hard, by a deterministic polynomial-time reduction, to distinguish two-to-one games of the following kinds:*\n\n1.  *there is a labeling of value at least $1-\\theta$;*\n\n2.  *every labeling has value at most $\\theta$.*\n\n*Every constraint is a total map $X\\to Y$ with exactly two preimages of each label in $Y$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-friedgut",
        "exact_quote": "For every $b\\ge0$ and $\\alpha>0$, there is a finite integer $J_{\\mathrm{unif}}(b,\\alpha)$ with the following property.         "source_id": "PM-102.5"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-evaluation-invariant",
        "exact_quote": "Every global game labeling $\\ell$ gives a well-defined real value on the rank vertices by $$b_{\\mathbf d}(x)\\longmapsto x(\\ell(\\mathbf d)),$$ where $\\ell(\\mathbf d)$ is the tuple of labels supplied by $\\ell$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-spreading",
        "exact_quote": "For every integer $L\\ge3$ and every $\\eta>0$, there are an integer $N\\ge L$ and a rational probability distribution on increasing maps $\\psi\\colon[L]\\hookrightarrow[N]$ such that, for all $I,I'\\in\\binom{[L]}{3}$, $$\\label{eq:rank-spreading-tv}\n  \\mathop{\\mathrm{TV}}\\bigl(\\mathcal L(\\psi(I)),\\mathcal L(\\psi(I'))\\bigr)\\le\\eta.$$ Here $\\mathcal L$ denotes the law of a random variable, and all the laws in Equation <a href=\"#eq:rank-spreading-tv\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:rank-spreading-tv\">[eq:rank-spreading-tv]</a> use the same random map $\\psi$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Rank graph** ([source](#stmt-def-rank-graph)): For $w\in U\cup V$, let $\Lambda_w=X$ if $w\in U$ and $\Lambda_w=Y$ if $w\in V$, and put $m_w=\lvert\Lambda_w\rvert$. The allowed vertex tuples are $$\bigcup_{t=0}^{T}U^t \quad\cup\quad \bigcup_{t=0}^{T-1}(U^t\times V).$$ Tuples are ordered sequences and may contain repeated vertices. For an allowed tuple $\mathbf d=(w_1,\ldots,w_t)$, let $\Lambda_{\mathbf d}=\prod_{j=1}^t\Lambda_{w_j}$; the product for the empty tuple is a singleton. In every case $$\lvert\Lambda_{\mathbf d}\rvert\le m^T. \label{eq:tuple-domain-bound}$$ For every function $x:\Lambda_{\mathbf d}\to[N]$, introduce a formal rank vertex $b_{\mathbf d}(x)$. Within each tuple, introduce an arc $b_{\mathbf d}(x)\to b_{\mathbf d}(y)$ whenever $$x(\lambda)<y(\lambda) \qquad\text{for every }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbf d}.$$ Whenever $\mathbf s w$ is an allowed extension of $\mathbf s$, identify $$b_{\mathbf s}(x) \equiv b_{\mathbf s w}(x\circ\operatorname{proj}), \label{eq:prefix-identification}$$ where $\operatorname{proj}:\Lambda_{\mathbf s w}\to\Lambda_{\mathbf s}$ drops the last coordinate. Take the equivalence relation generated by all these identifications and keep the induced arcs between its classes. Each resulting rank vertex has cost $D+1$, independently of the number of its formal representatives. We use the same notation for a formal vertex and its equivalence class. The vertices represented by the constant profiles with value $j$ are denoted by $o_j$, for $j\in[N]$.
- **Wildcard vertex** ([source](#stmt-def-wildcard)): Fix an allowed tuple $\mathbf d$, an increasing map $\psi$, an ordered partition $C_1,\ldots,C_k$ of $\Lambda_{\mathbf d}$ with $1\le k\le k_{\max}$, and a set $Z\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf d}$, called the star set. Empty cells in the partition are permitted. Put $z_\ell=\psi(10\ell+10)$ for $\ell\in[k]$. The corresponding wildcard vertex has an incoming arc from every rank vertex $b_{\mathbf d}(x)$ for which $$x(\lambda)<z_\ell \quad\text{for all }\ell\in[k] \text{ and }\lambda\in C_\ell\setminus Z, \label{eq:wildcard-incoming}$$ and an outgoing arc to every $b_{\mathbf d}(x)$ satisfying the strict reverse inequalities $$x(\lambda)>z_\ell \quad\text{for all }\ell\in[k] \text{ and }\lambda\in C_\ell\setminus Z. \label{eq:wildcard-outgoing}$$ There are no restrictions on the values of $x$ on $Z$.
- **Prefix data** ([source](#stmt-def-prefix-data)): For $t\in\{0,\ldots,T\}$, draw $\mathbf s=(u_1,\ldots,u_t)$ independently from $\nu_U$ in each slot. Independently draw $i_1,\ldots,i_t$ uniformly from $[M]$. Conditional on these indices, draw $P_j\subseteq X$ using independent Bernoulli $p_{i_j}$ membership bits, independently for different slots $j$. Partition $\Lambda_{\mathbf s}$ by the membership patterns $$\bigl(\mathbf 1\{\lambda_j\in P_j\}\bigr)_{j=1}^t, \qquad \lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbf s}.$$ Order the $2^t$ cells lexicographically with $1$ before $0$, keeping cells that happen to be empty, and denote them by $C_1,\ldots,C_{2^t}$. Put $$C_{<r}=\bigcup_{\ell<r}C_\ell, \qquad C_{\le r}=\bigcup_{\ell\le r}C_\ell.$$ At $t=0$ this is the one-cell partition of the singleton product domain.
- **Test families** ([source](#stmt-item-comparison-test)): Add the following families of test vertices to the rank graph. In each family, independently draw $\psi$ from the fixed rank distribution. Each outcome is a separate vertex with cost equal to its probability multiplied by the stated total mass. Distinct outcomes remain separate even if they produce the same incident arcs. 1. **Full-prefix tests, of total mass $H$.** Draw prefix data at length $T$ and use the wildcard vertex in tuple $\mathbf s$ with those $2^T$ cells. Its star set is $$Z=\left\{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbf s}: \text{there exists }i\in[M]\text{ such that } \#\{j:i_j=i,\ \lambda_j\in P_j\} <\frac{p_iT}{2M}\right\}. \label{eq:full-prefix-stars}$$ 2. **Single-label tests, with one family on each side.** The big-side family has total mass $\lvert X\rvert$, and the small-side family has total mass $\lvert Y\rvert$. Choose $t$ uniformly from $\{0,\ldots,T-1\}$ and draw prefix data at length $t$. Independently draw $w$ from the endpoint marginal on the chosen side, a uniformly random label $j_0\in\Lambda_w$, and a uniformly random permutation $(\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(m_w))$ of $\Lambda_w$. Use a wildcard vertex in tuple $\mathbf s w$. Its cells, in order, are $$C_\ell\times\{\sigma(a)\}, \qquad \ell=1,\ldots,2^t,\quad a=1,\ldots,m_w,$$ with the prefix-cell index first. Thus the same label permutation is used in every prefix cell. Its star set is $Z=\Lambda_{\mathbf s}\times\{j_0\}$. 3. **Comparison tests, of total mass $K$.** Choose $t$ uniformly from $\{0,\ldots,T-1\}$ and draw prefix data at that length. Independently draw an edge $e=(u,v)$ from $\nu$ and an index $i$ uniformly from $[M]$. Conditional on $t$, choose $r$ uniformly from $[2^t]$, independently of all other choices. Draw $R_u\subseteq X$ and $R_v\subseteq Y$ by the following coupling. Independently in each block indexed by $y\in Y$, draw the two bits on $\pi_e^{-1}(y)$ independently with probability $p_i$ of being one. If either is one, set the bit at $y$ to one. If both are zero, set it to one with probability $$\frac{p_i^2}{(1-p_i)^2}. \label{eq:coupling-residual}$$ This is a valid probability because $p_i\le 1/4$. The marginal probability of the bit at $y$ being one is $$1-(1-p_i)^2+(1-p_i)^2\frac{p_i^2}{(1-p_i)^2} =2p_i=q_i.$$ Hence the two complete marginals are the product measures of biases $p_i$ and $q_i$, respectively. Different projection blocks are independent, and $$R_u\subseteq\pi_e^{-1}(R_v) \label{eq:coupling-inclusion}$$ holds for every outcome. These bits are fresh, independent of the prefix subsets. For $w\in\{u,v\}$ and $R\subseteq\Lambda_w$, define $$E_w^r(R)= (C_{<r}\times\Lambda_w)\cup(C_r\times R). \label{eq:comparison-sets}$$ The test vertex has just the incoming and outgoing arcs in the path $$b_{\mathbf s v}^{\psi}(E_v^r(R_v);2,17) \ \longrightarrow\ \text{test}\ \longrightarrow\ b_{\mathbf s u}^{\psi}(E_u^r(R_u);3,18). \label{eq:comparison-path}$$
- **Good tuples** ([source](#stmt-def-good-tuple)): A triple $(a,c,h)$ is *usable* if $$\label{eq:usable-triples} 5\le c\le L-5,\qquad 2\le a<c,\qquad c+2\le h\le L.$$ The tuple $\mathbf d$ is *good at $\psi$* if $$G_{\mathbf d}(E;a,c,h)=g_{\mathbf d}(E)$$ for every $E\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf d}$ and every usable triple. Otherwise it is *bad at $\psi$*.
- **Conditioned functions** ([source](#stmt-def-conditioned-functions)): For each game vertex $w$, fix a label $\lambda_w^\circ\in\Lambda_w$ independently of all the sampled data. Given $\mathcal D$, let $C_1,\ldots,C_{2^t}$ be its ordered prefix partition. If $\mathbf s$ is good, define its transition index by $$r_*=\min\{r:g_{\mathbf s}(C_{\le r})=1\}.$$ For every $w\in U\cup V$ define a function on $2^{\Lambda_w}$ by $$f_w(R)= \begin{cases} g_{\mathbf s w}\bigl(E_w^{r_*}(R)\bigr), &\text{if $\mathbf s w$ is good},\\ \mathbf 1\{\lambda_w^\circ\in R\}, &\text{otherwise}, \end{cases}$$ where, as in Equation <a href="#eq:comparison-sets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-sets">[eq:comparison-sets]</a>, $$E_w^r(R) =(C_{<r}\times\Lambda_w)\cup(C_r\times R).$$ When the dependence on the shared data needs to be displayed, write $f_{w,\mathcal D}$ in place of $f_w$. For $0<p<1$, let $$h_w(p) =\mathbb E_{R\sim\mu_p^{\Lambda_w}} f_w(R),$$ and similarly write $h_{w,\mathcal D}(p)$ when needed. This expectation is taken with the function fixed.
- **Pivotality budget** ([source](#stmt-def-pivotal-budget)): Let $S$ be a nonempty finite set, and let $f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ be monotone with $f(\varnothing)=0$ and $f(S)=1$. For each $j\in S$, let $\sigma_j$ be a uniform permutation of $S\setminus\{j\}$. Write $R_a(\sigma_j)$ for the set of its first $a$ elements, where $0\le a\le \|S\|-1$. Define $$B(f)=\sum_{j\in S} \Pr_{\sigma_j}\!\left[ \begin{gathered} \text{there exists }a\in\{0,\ldots,\|S\|-1\}\text{ such that}\\ f(R_a(\sigma_j))=0,\quad f(R_a(\sigma_j)\cup\{j\})=1 \end{gathered} \right].$$ Thus both the empty prefix and the full prefix $S\setminus\{j\}$ are allowed. A permutation contributes only once for a given $j$, even when several of its prefixes witness pivotality. In particular, $0\le B(f)\le \|S\|$.
- **Directed feedback vertex set** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A directed feedback vertex set of a finite digraph $G$ is a set $F\subseteq V(G)$ for which the induced digraph $G-F$ is acyclic.
- **Junta** ([source](#pm-h-9)): A function is a *junta on $J\subseteq S$* if its value depends only on $R\cap J$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-cor-dyadic-junta) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-dyadic-junta): AND-block lifting followed by conditional Boolean prediction. -> Dyadic junta approximation.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-spreading) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-spreading): Finite Ramsey coloring, finite minimax, and rational approximation with strict slack. -> Rational rank-embedding law.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-polynomial) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-polynomial): Explicit enumeration of rational outcomes and quotienting prefix identifications. -> Polynomial weighted graph construction.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-prop-completeness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-completeness): Delete starred wildcard outcomes and unsatisfied-edge comparisons; extend the increasing labeling evaluation. -> Low-cost feedback vertex set.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-goodness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-goodness): Compare ordered bits and union-bound subset and usable-triple disagreements. -> Sampled tuple badness bound.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-lem-prefix-refinement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prefix-refinement): Use exact prefix lifts and lexicographic refinement of the transition cell. -> Membership bits equal conditioned function outputs.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-prefix-lower-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-prefix-lower-bound): Force deletion of starred transition cells and average the nonnegative membership count. -> Acceptance lower bound at every scale.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-pivotal-inequalities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pivotal-inequalities): Couple product subsets to independent arrival times and charge possible permutation witnesses. -> Bias-independent influence and acceptance budget.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-prop-pivotal-budget) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-pivotal-budget): Charge each label once to its single-label test and bound exceptional extensions. -> Expected budget bound on both endpoint marginals.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-lem-comparison-charge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-comparison-charge): Hit the shared transition cell and force deletion when comparison endpoints reverse the surviving order. -> Coupled disagreement bound.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-lem-list-decoding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-list-decoding): Randomly label from fixed lists and compare the expected satisfied-edge fraction to game value. -> Projection-overlap probability bound.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-prop-product-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-product-comparison): Approximate by juntas, discard high-budget and intersecting-list events, then use independent projection fibers. -> Product comparison bound.
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-prop-weighted-soundness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weighted-soundness): Sum dyadic tail contributions for the same pivotality random variable. -> No feedback vertex set within the soundness cost threshold.
- **R14** [claim](#stmt-lem-round-and-clone) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-round-and-clone): Round positive rational costs upward and replace each vertex by an independent cluster. -> Exact rounded deletion optimum and controlled gap loss.
- **R15** [claim](#stmt-cor-feedback-arc) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-feedback-arc): Split original vertices and use private edge-disjoint connector paths; recover a vertex deletion set. -> Approximation-preserving feedback arc set hardness.

For every $b\\ge0$ and $\\alpha>0$, there is a finite integer $J(p,b,\\alpha)$, independent of $\\lvert S\\rvert$, such that every Boolean function $f:2^S\\to\\{0,1\\}$ with $I_p(f)\\le b$ has a Boolean junta approximant on at most $J(p,b,\\alpha)$ coordinates with error at most $\\alpha$ under $\\mu_p^S$.",

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-goodness",
          "exact_quote": "For each fixed allowed tuple $\\mathbf d$, $$\\label{eq:goodness-probability}\n  \\Pr_{\\psi}[\\mathbf d\\text{ is bad at }\\psi]\n  \\le 3\\eta L^3 2^{\\lvert\\Lambda_{\\mathbf d}\\rvert}\n  \\le\\rho.$$ The same upper bound $\\rho$ holds when $\\mathbf d$ is sampled independently of $\\psi$.",
          "source_id": "PM-102.5"
        }
      ],

      "inputs": [
        "Fixed surviving order and independent embedding."
      ],
      "method": "Compare ordered bits and union-bound subset and usable-triple disagreements.",
      "output": "Sampled tuple badness bound.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-goodness"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-prefix-refinement",
      "depends_on": [
        "R5"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-prefix-refinement",
          "exact_quote": "Draw prefix data of length $T$ and an independent embedding $\\psi$. Write $\\mathbf s_j=(u_1,\\ldots,u_j)$, and let $C^{(j)}_1,\\ldots,C^{(j)}_{2^j}$ be the partition at depth $j$. For $j=0$, this is the single cell $\\Lambda_{\\varnothing}$. Suppose that $\\mathbf s_T$ is good, and let $\\tau_j$ be the transition index at depth $j$. Then the full transition cell is nonempty and, for every $0\\le j\\le T$, $$C^{(T)}_{\\tau_T}\n  \\subseteq C^{(j)}_{\\tau_j}\\times X^{T-j}.$$ Let $\\mathcal D_{j-1}$ denote the shared data consisting of the same embedding, the first $j-1$ prefix slots, and the deterministic length $j-1$. For each $1\\le j\\le T$, the membership bit of slot $j$ throughout the full transition cell is $$\\mathbf 1\\{\\lambda_j\\in P_j\\}\n  =f_{u_j,\\mathcal D_{j-1}}(P_j)\n  \\qquad\n  \\text{for every }\n  (\\lambda_1,\\ldots,\\lambda_T)\\in C^{(T)}_{\\tau_T}.\n  \\label{eq:transition-membership}$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **clone-optimum**: Independent clusters with complete intercluster arcs preserve the minimum rounded deletion cost exactly.
- **rounding-gap**: Rounding upward controls the additive cost increase and transfers the weighted completeness and soundness gap.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **variable-factor**: Treat the fixed-factor hardness reduction as an efficient construction when the approximation factor is part of the input. -> The construction constants and polynomial may depend on the target factor.
- **partial-clusters**: Translate every partially deleted clone cluster into a deleted original vertex. -> A surviving clone can still lift an original cycle; only fully deleted clusters justify the recovery.
- **hardness-as-algorithm**: Use the hardness theorem as an improved approximation algorithm or an explicit growth rate for the necessary factor. -> The theorem excludes fixed constant guarantees without giving that rate or improving the general algorithm.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The rounding-and-cloning lemma explicitly constructs a finite unweighted digraph and recovers feedback vertex sets from completely deleted clusters.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON n, arcs, weights as [positive numerator, positive denominator].
# PRECONDITIONS: 1 <= n <= 4; distinct loopless arcs; positive costs; at most 8 clones.
# OUTPUTS: Exact cluster counts, expanded arcs, minimum deletion size, deletion witness.
# TIME: O(2^Q * (Q + Ehat)) graph operations plus exact rational arithmetic.
# SPACE: O(Q + Ehat) graph storage, where Q is the total clone count.
# ARITHMETIC: Certified exact python-flint fmpq/fmpz rational and integer arithmetic.
# FAILURE: Reject malformed inputs or instances exceeding the finite admission bound.
import json, sys
from itertools import combinations
from flint import fmpq

def solve(data):
    n = data['n']
    assert type(n) is int and 1 <= n <= 4
    arcs = [tuple(e) for e in data['arcs']]
    assert len(set(arcs)) == len(arcs)
    assert all(len(e) == 2 and all(type(x) is int and 0 <= x < n for x in e) and e[0] != e[1] for e in arcs)
    assert len(data['weights']) == n
    counts = []
    for pair in data['weights']:
        assert len(pair) == 2 and all(type(x) is int and x > 0 for x in pair)
        scaled = n * fmpq(pair[0], pair[1])
        count = (int(scaled.p) + int(scaled.q) - 1) // int(scaled.q)
        counts.append(count)
    total = sum(counts)
    assert total <= 8
    clusters = []
    offset = 0
    for c in counts:
        clusters.append(list(range(offset, offset + c)))
        offset += c
    expanded = sorted((a, b) for u, v in arcs for a in clusters[u] for b in clusters[v])
    def acyclic(deleted):
        alive = set(range(total)) - set(deleted)
        indegree = {v: 0 for v in alive}
        adjacency = {v: [] for v in alive}
        for a, b in expanded:
            if a in alive and b in alive:
                indegree[b] += 1
                adjacency[a].append(b)
        queue = [v for v in alive if indegree[v] == 0]
        seen = 0
        while queue:
            v = queue.pop()
            seen += 1
            for w in adjacency[v]:
                indegree[w] -= 1
                if indegree[w] == 0:
                    queue.append(w)
        return seen == len(alive)
    for size in range(total + 1):
        for deleted in combinations(range(total), size):
            if acyclic(deleted):
                answer = {'counts': counts, 'arcs': expanded, 'optimum': size, 'deleted': deleted}
                return answer
    raise AssertionError('finite search exhausted')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    print(json.dumps(solve(json.loads(sys.stdin.read())), sort_keys=True))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A graph transformation component can adapt the stated rounding-and-cloning construction; this is a finite reduction component rather than a feedback-set optimiser. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Gameplay and simulation contracts are ABSENT. The games are finite labeling constraint instances used as the source of a hardness reduction. |
| tools | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | A feedback-set validation tool can adapt the stated topological-order certificate for the remaining finite digraph. |
| algorithms | True | strong | adaptable | stated | The paper states deterministic polynomial reductions for fixed approximation factor and constructive maps between feedback vertex and feedback arc solutions. |
| data_structures | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The rank graph gives a finite quotient representation whose prefix identifications preserve evaluation. Its use as a reusable data structure is an adaptation of that mathematical construction. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Hardware execution, accelerator mapping, and hardware resource bounds are ABSENT; the stated implementation is a deterministic finite graph construction. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Cryptographic primitives and security reductions are ABSENT; the hardness result concerns approximation of directed cycle deletion. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A learning algorithm, training contract, and generalisation guarantee are ABSENT. Junta approximants serve the soundness argument and need not be monotone. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | Directed cycle deletion is an explicit cardinality or positive rational cost minimisation problem. The theorem constrains constant-factor approximation on general unweighted digraphs. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A distributed protocol, communication model, and fault tolerance contract are ABSENT; the source specifies finite graph reductions. |
| other (Finite Boolean-function analysis) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Finite Boolean-function analysis supplies a bias-independent permutation pivotality budget. Exact finite evaluation may support analysis of a given truth table, but the source does not supply an efficient general evaluation bound. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-fixed-factor** (approximation): Polynomial construction is asserted with the approximation factor fixed; efficient dependence on a variable factor is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Treating the reduction as an efficient algorithm with a variable approximation parameter.']
- **L-growth-rate** (approximation): The hardness result does not determine how the required approximation factor grows with input size or improve the general approximation algorithm. Blocked use: ['Extracting an explicit growth rate or a better approximation algorithm.']
- **L-graph-scope** (hypothesis): The graph conventions require finite loopless digraphs and permit opposite arcs. Blocked use: ['Applying the stated constructions unchanged to graphs with self-loops or infinite vertex sets.']
- **L-rational-positive-costs** (hypothesis): Rounding and cloning require positive rational vertex costs and a loopless input graph. Blocked use: ['Using empty clusters arising from zero costs or applying the independent-cluster construction to self-loops.']
- **L-fixed-cost-bound** (hypothesis): The polynomial size guarantee for cloning assumes a fixed upper cost bound. Blocked use: ['Claiming the same polynomial bound for an unrestricted cost bound supplied as part of the input.']
- **L-junta-monotonicity** (edge_case): Junta approximants need not be monotone; the reduction uses their errors and coordinate supports. Blocked use: ['Assuming that an approximating junta inherits monotonicity.']
- **L-edge-independent-lists** (hypothesis): Coordinate lists must be chosen without information about the freshly sampled edge. Blocked use: ['Using edge-dependent coordinate lists as a game labeling in the soundness argument.']
- **L-common-tail-law** (hypothesis): The tail contradiction concerns a common pivotality variable under a fixed probability law across bias scales. Blocked use: ['Summing tail bounds for unrelated functions or changing laws across scales.']
- **L-rank-preprocessing** (formal_scope): The rank distribution may require exhaustive finite search as preprocessing depending on the fixed factor. Blocked use: ['Treating existence of the rank distribution as a practical size or running-time guarantee.']
- **L-pivotal-functions** (hypothesis): Arrival-time inequalities require a monotone Boolean function on a nonempty finite set, with bottom value zero and top value one. Blocked use: ['Applying the pivotality inequalities to arbitrary nonmonotone truth tables.']
- **L-sampled-goodness** (formal_scope): The goodness estimate is used for sampled tuples; simultaneous goodness of every tuple is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Replacing a sampled-tuple bound by simultaneous goodness without a further argument.']
- **L-exact-projection-fibers** (hypothesis): The starting game uses total projections with exactly two preimages for each small-side label. Blocked use: ['Transferring the comparison coupling unchanged to arbitrary projection fiber sizes.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 102 (`lean/docs/102.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The Unique Games Theorem](../../preprints/The-Unique-Games-Theorem-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A Direct Proof of Optimal Max-Cut Hardness](../../preprints/A-Direct-Proof-of-Optimal-Max-Cut-Hardness-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [The Factor-Two Hardness Threshold for Vertex Cover](../../preprints/The-Factor-Two-Hardness-Threshold-for-Vertex-Cover-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Constant-factor hardness of Min-UnCut](../../preprints/Constant-factor-hardness-of-Min-UnCut-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Constant-factor hardness of directed feedback vertex set](../../preprints/Constant-factor-hardness-of-directed-feedback-vertex-set-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Unique Games conjecture asks for hardness of distinguishing nearly satisfiable unique games from games of very small value. The formalized result gives, for every fixed $0<\varepsilon,\delta<1/2$, a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from binary 3SAT to nonempty unweighted simple bipartite unique games. Satisfiable inputs have value at least $1-\varepsilon$ and unsatisfiable inputs have value at most $\delta$. The alphabet is a fixed $\mathbb F_2^s$ depending only on the errors, and every constraint is a translation.
>
> The formalized result establishes hardness of approximating Max-Cut beyond the Goemans–Williamson constant $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}$. For every fixed $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}<\alpha\le1$, it gives a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from binary 3SAT to a strictly separated Max-Cut gap on finite simple unweighted graphs. The reduction includes the explicit positive integer scaling used in the gap statement.
>
> The formalized result gives the factor-two hardness threshold for Vertex Cover. For each integer $m\ge4$, a polynomial-time reduction from binary 3SAT produces simple unweighted graphs with cover density below $1/2+1/m$ on satisfiable inputs and above $1-1/m$ on unsatisfiable inputs. Consequently, any polynomial-time approximation with a fixed factor $1\le\alpha<2$ would give a polynomial-time decision algorithm for 3SAT. No assumption that $P\ne NP$ is built into the statement.
>
> Min-UnCut minimizes the number of edges left uncut by a bipartition. The formalization constructs a deterministic reduction from encoded $3$-SAT formulas to finite simple unweighted Min-UnCut instances with arbitrarily large fixed multiplicative gaps. For every integer $K\ge2$, satisfiable formulas give optimum at most the output threshold, while unsatisfiable formulas give optimum strictly greater than $K$ times that threshold. Runtime and output length are polynomial for each fixed $K$, establishing hardness for every fixed approximation factor greater than one.
>
> A directed feedback vertex set meets every directed cycle. The formalization proves hardness of approximating the minimum such set within any fixed constant factor. For every real factor $A\ge1$ and every language in NP, it constructs a polynomial-time gap reduction to unweighted directed graphs with the stated completeness and soundness separation. Thus a fixed-factor polynomial-time approximation would imply a polynomial-time algorithm for every NP language.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace DirectedFeedback

/-- Unary self-delimiting natural numbers. Outputs are explicit, not succinct. -/
def natBits (n : ℕ) : List Bool := List.replicate n true ++ [false]

/-- The explicit framing used for an input and its NP certificate. -/
def pairBits (p : List Bool × List Bool) : List Bool :=
  natBits p.1.length ++ p.1 ++ p.2

/-- Finitely many stacks and control states are already part of `FinTM2`.
This additionally excludes infinite stack alphabets. -/
def FiniteAlphabet (M : Turing.FinTM2) : Prop := ∀ k, Finite (M.Γ k)

/-- Polynomially bounded certificates checked by an actual deterministic
finite-alphabet machine, on the explicitly encoded input/certificate pair. -/
structure NPVerifier where
  witnessBound : Polynomial ℕ
  verify : (List Bool × List Bool) → Bool
  computation : Turing.TM2ComputableInPolyTime pairBits (fun b : Bool => [b]) verify
  finiteAlphabet : FiniteAlphabet computation.tm

def NPVerifier.Accepts (V : NPVerifier) (input : List Bool) : Prop :=
  ∃ witness : List Bool, witness.length ≤ V.witnessBound.eval input.length ∧
    V.verify (input, witness) = true

/-- NP is defined independently of SAT or any hardness assertion. -/
def InNP (language : List Bool → Prop) : Prop :=
  ∃ V : NPVerifier, ∀ input, language input ↔ V.Accepts input

/-- Finite loopless digraph. Opposite arcs are permitted. Every arc is listed
explicitly. No symmetric closure or undirected-cycle convention is used. -/
structure Digraph where
  n : ℕ
  arcs : List (Fin n × Fin n)
  loopless : ∀ e ∈ arcs, e.1 ≠ e.2
  nodup : arcs.Nodup

namespace Digraph

/-- An actual simple directed cycle of positive length. A cyclic enumeration
of `r + 1` distinct vertices supplies every successive arc, including the
closing arc. A 2-cycle is allowed; looplessness rules out length 1. -/
def IsCycle (G : Digraph) {r : ℕ} (c : Fin (r + 1) → Fin G.n) : Prop :=
  Function.Injective c ∧ ∀ i, (c i, c (i + 1)) ∈ G.arcs

/-- A feedback set intersects EVERY actual directed cycle. -/
def Feedback (G : Digraph) (F : Finset (Fin G.n)) : Prop :=
  ∀ (r : ℕ) (c : Fin (r + 1) → Fin G.n), G.IsCycle c → ∃ i, c i ∈ F

/-- Minimum number of vertices of a feedback set. No weights or auxiliary
cost oracle appear in this definition. -/
noncomputable def dfvs (G : Digraph) : ℕ := by
  classical
  exact Nat.find (show ∃ k : ℕ, ∃ F : Finset (Fin G.n), G.Feedback F ∧ F.card = k from
    ⟨G.n, Finset.univ, by
      intro r c _
      exact ⟨0, Finset.mem_univ _⟩, by simp⟩)

/-- Full explicit vertex and arc serialization, including isolated vertices. -/
def bits (G : Digraph) : List Bool :=
  ([G.n] ++ List.range G.n ++ [G.arcs.length] ++
    G.arcs.flatMap (fun e => [e.1.val, e.2.val])).flatMap natBits

end Digraph

/-- Output includes a strictly positive integer threshold. -/
structure GapInstance where
  graph : Digraph
  k : ℕ
  k_pos : 0 < k

def GapInstance.bits (I : GapInstance) : List Bool :=
  natBits I.k ++ I.graph.bits

/-- A strict YES/NO gap reduction implemented by a finite-alphabet machine
with polynomial runtime in the raw input bit length. -/
structure GapReduction (A : ℝ) (language : List Bool → Prop) where
  construct : List Bool → GapInstance
  computation : Turing.TM2ComputableInPolyTime (id : List Bool → List Bool)
    GapInstance.bits construct
  finiteAlphabet : FiniteAlphabet computation.tm
  completeness : ∀ input, language input →
    (construct input).graph.dfvs ≤ (construct input).k
  soundness : ∀ input, ¬ language input →
    A * ((construct input).k : ℝ) < ((construct input).graph.dfvs : ℝ)

/-- Constant-factor directed feedback vertex set hardness for all NP languages. -/
def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∀ A : ℝ, 1 ≤ A → ∀ language : List Bool → Prop,
    InNP language → Nonempty (GapReduction A language)

end DirectedFeedback

theorem DirectedFeedback.main : DirectedFeedback.MainStatement := by
  sorry

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Approximating minimum directed feedback vertex set within any fixed constant factor is NP-hard, even on unweighted digraphs.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A directed feedback vertex set of a finite digraph $G$ is a set $F\subseteq V(G)$ for which the induced digraph $G-F$ is acyclic. Write $$\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)=\min\{\lvert F\rvert:G-F\text{ is acyclic}\}.$$ Equivalently, $F$ intersects every directed cycle. A topological order of $G-F$ certifies this property in polynomial time. The minimum-cost version assigns a positive rational cost to each vertex and minimizes the sum of the deleted costs. Throughout, digraphs are finite and loopless; two vertices may have arcs in both directions. An $A$-approximation, for $A\ge1$, returns a feedback vertex set of size at most $A\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The approximation question

Writing $n=\lvert V(G)\rvert$, the general $O(\log n\log\log n)$ approximation guarantee originates in Seymour’s work on fractional packing of directed circuits (Seymour 1995) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Seymour1995](#ref-Seymour1995)]. Even, Naor, Schieber, and Sudan give a constructive algorithm covering weighted directed feedback sets (Even et al. 1998) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Even1998](#ref-Even1998)].

Ordinary NP-hardness of approximation already follows from Vertex Cover, which asks for a smallest vertex set meeting every edge of an undirected graph. Replacing every edge of an undirected graph by its two opposite arcs makes a vertex set a directed feedback vertex set exactly when it is a vertex cover. Thus the earlier hardness factors below $10\sqrt5-21$ of Dinur and Safra (Dinur and Safra 2005) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[DinurSafra2005](#ref-DinurSafra2005)], and the factors strictly below $\sqrt2$ obtained by the completed two-to-one games line (Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2018, sec. 1.3.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KMS2018](#ref-KMS2018)], transfer to DFVS. These results exclude particular constants; excluding every constant had required a stronger complexity assumption.

Guruswami, Manokaran, and Raghavendra established the Unique-Games barrier against every constant factor through maximum acyclic subgraph and feedback arc set (Guruswami, Manokaran, and Raghavendra 2008) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GMR2008](#ref-GMR2008)]. The expanded ordering-constraint framework appears in the journal paper with Håstad and Charikar (Guruswami et al. 2011) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[GHMRC2011](#ref-GHMRC2011)]. Svensson gave a direct Unique-Games-based construction with strong structure in the YES case (Svensson 2013) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Svensson2013](#ref-Svensson2013)]. Guruswami and Lee subsequently gave a simpler proof with structured completeness and short cycles in every sufficiently large induced subgraph in the NO case (Guruswami and Lee 2016) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[GuruswamiLee2016](#ref-GuruswamiLee2016)]. The recent discussion of Ghorbani and Mnich records the remaining gap for general digraphs (Ghorbani and Mnich 2026) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[GhorbaniMnich2026](#ref-GhorbaniMnich2026)].

We prove the corresponding exclusion of every constant factor from ordinary NP-hardness.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every fixed real constant $A\ge1$, it is NP-hard to approximate minimum directed feedback vertex set within factor $A$ on unweighted digraphs. More precisely, there is a deterministic polynomial-time reduction from an NP-hard promise problem to instances $(G,k)$ with $k\in\mathbb N_{>0}$. The polynomial and its constants may depend on $A$. The reduction has the following alternatives: $$\textnormal{YES: }\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)\le k,
  \qquad
  \textnormal{NO: }\mathop{\mathrm{DFVS}}(G)>Ak.$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> implies that a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm with any constant approximation guarantee exists if and only if $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$. For the reverse implication, membership of the size-bounded decision problem in NP permits exact optimization and witness search when $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$. The theorem establishes that the required approximation factor grows without bound with the input size, assuming $\mathrm P\ne\mathrm{NP}$. It does not identify that growth rate or improve the general approximation algorithm.

A directed feedback arc set is a set of arcs whose deletion leaves an acyclic digraph. Corollary <a href="#cor:feedback-arc" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:feedback-arc">31</a> transfers the same hardness to this problem on unweighted simple loopless digraphs, through a reduction preserving both the optimum and approximation solutions. The vertex-set hardness also holds when opposite arcs are forbidden, as shown in Remark <a href="#rem:no-opposite-arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:no-opposite-arcs">30</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Methodological background

Our construction shares the useful division between protected vertices and deletable test vertices in Svensson’s dictatorship gadget (Svensson 2013, sec. 3.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Svensson2013](#ref-Svensson2013)]. His soundness proof also reads a Boolean function from a topological order (Svensson 2013, sec. 3.1.3) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Svensson2013](#ref-Svensson2013)]. Here the protected vertices encode rank profiles, and compatibility across tuple prefixes supplies a family of monotone functions. Svensson uses the “It Ain’t Over Till It’s Over” theorem; Guruswami and Lee instead use an invariance principle (Guruswami and Lee 2016, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GuruswamiLee2016](#ref-GuruswamiLee2016)]. Our analytic input is Friedgut’s theorem on Boolean functions of bounded total influence (Friedgut 1998) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Friedgut1998](#ref-Friedgut1998)]. We derive its required fixed-bias form explicitly. Junta-based label decoding already appears in Dinur and Safra’s Vertex Cover hardness proof (Dinur and Safra 2005, sec. 1.1 and 3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DinurSafra2005](#ref-DinurSafra2005)], where a nearby bias is selected to obtain bounded total influence. Here a single pivotality budget controls all the prescribed biases, and the contradiction requires their estimates to concern the same distribution of functions.

The rank distribution is obtained by combining finite Ramsey theory (Ramsey 1930) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Ramsey1930](#ref-Ramsey1930)] with finite minimax (Neumann 1928) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[vonNeumann1928](#ref-vonNeumann1928)]. These are the external combinatorial tools in the rank-spreading argument. The shared-prefix construction and the permutation pivotality budget are proved below. Their role is to compare many biases without changing the distribution of the functions being analyzed. The source of hardness is the published imperfect-completeness two-to-one theorem, so the proof does not consume a Unique Games hardness theorem.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The reduction and its new ingredients

A two-to-one game has a bipartite constraint graph with a label set $X$ at each big-side vertex and a label set $Y$ at each small-side vertex. Each edge carries a map $X\to Y$ with exactly two preimages per label; an edge is satisfied when the chosen endpoint labels obey that map. The game value is the largest fraction of satisfied edges, measured under its prescribed edge distribution. Our starting point is the imperfect-completeness 2-to-1 Games Theorem, obtained through the work of Dinur, Khot, Kindler, Minzer, and Safra, Khot, Minzer, and Safra, and Barak, Kothari, and Steurer (Dinur et al. 2025; Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2018; Barak, Kothari, and Steurer 2019) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[DKKMS2025](#ref-DKKMS2025), [KMS2018](#ref-KMS2018), [BKS2019](#ref-BKS2019)]. We use its precise form in Theorem <a href="#thm:games" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:games">2</a>: for arbitrarily small constant $\theta>0$, distinguishing game value at least $1-\theta$ from value at most $\theta$ is NP-hard, with fixed alphabets and exact two-element projection fibers. The perfect-completeness 2-to-1 Games Conjecture is not a premise of the argument.

The central difficulty is to extract a game labeling from an arbitrary acyclic remainder. An acyclic graph supplies a topological order, but its order can interleave the many vertices representing the same local test. The proof first makes comparisons within that order consistent, then interprets them as Boolean functions. Small deletion cost will constrain these functions in incompatible ways.

For a fixed integer factor $A$, put $D=10A$. We construct a weighted graph with a feedback vertex set of cost at most $4$ in the YES case. Suppose, toward the soundness contradiction, that a NO instance has one of cost at most $D$. Fix a topological order after its deletion. The following four steps analyze this arbitrary order. A Boolean function on subsets is evaluated at bias $p$ by including each label independently with probability $p$; its acceptance is the probability that its value is one.

1.  *Rank comparisons with a common interpretation.* For each short tuple of game vertices, we create vertices indexed by rank-valued functions of label tuples. A global labeling evaluates all these functions consistently. In the soundness analysis, a putative inexpensive feedback set leaves every rank vertex present, so its surviving topological order supplies comparison bits. A finite Ramsey and minimax argument produces a distribution on rank embeddings for which all triples of positions have nearly identical image laws. Ordered comparisons then agree with high probability, yielding a monotone Boolean function of subsets of label tuples.

2.  *Conditioning through a shared prefix.* Successive random subsets split label tuples into lexicographically ordered cells. Monotonicity selects a unique transition cell, and prefix identifications make these transitions consistent under refinement. A full-prefix test prevents the chosen cell from having too few membership-one steps at any of many dyadic biases. On averaging, the conditioned big-side Boolean function has acceptance at least a constant multiple of the bias at every scale. The same prefix data define functions for every game vertex before a fresh constraint is drawn.

3.  *A pivotality budget independent of the alphabet size.* A single-label test charges the event that the starred label can be pivotal somewhere along a random permutation. The resulting quantity $B(f)$ is the sum, over labels, of the probability that the label is pivotal at some prefix of a random permutation of the other labels. It bounds both total influence and acceptance probability divided by the bias, at every bias. Its expectation is at most $D+1$ on either side of the game. Friedgut’s junta theorem (Friedgut 1998) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Friedgut1998](#ref-Friedgut1998)], transferred to fixed dyadic biases, therefore supplies bounded coordinate lists outside a small exceptional set. Their size is fixed before choosing the game alphabets.

4.  *A common tail variable across all scales.* A projection-respecting coupling compares bias $p$ on the big side with bias $2p$ on the small side. Game soundness makes the two junta lists typically use disjoint projection fibers. The corresponding outputs are then independent under the coupling, allowing the comparison bound to pass to product measures. At each dyadic bias, this forces a positive contribution of constant size to the tail sum of the small-side pivotality budget. All these estimates concern the same random variable under the same distribution. Adding sufficiently many such contributions contradicts its expected budget.

The pivotality quantity is independent of the bias. This is essential: a separate influence bound at each scale would not justify adding the contributions to one tail sum. Likewise, the shared prefix fixes all vertex functions before the tested game edge is drawn; otherwise the coordinate lists would not define a game labeling.

The proof uses finite probability spaces to specify weighted test families. Every outcome is included explicitly with its probability as part of its cost. Thus the reduction is deterministic. All parameters, alphabets, tuple lengths, and rank tables are constants once the target factor is fixed, even though some are extremely large. Finally, rounding each vertex cost upward and replacing a vertex by an independent cluster produces an unweighted gap.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> states the external theorems and proves the required dyadic junta corollary. Section <a href="#sec:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:construction">3</a> gives the parameter choices, graph, tests, and completeness argument. Section <a href="#sec:ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:ranks">4</a> proves rank spreading and the order lemmas used to read Boolean functions from a surviving graph. Sections <a href="#sec:prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prefix">5</a> and <a href="#sec:pivotality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pivotality">6</a> establish the acceptance lower bounds and pivotality budgets. Section <a href="#sec:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:soundness">7</a> gives the comparison and tail-sum contradiction. Section <a href="#sec:unweighting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:unweighting">8</a> removes the costs and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Games and Boolean functions

For a finite set $S$, we identify $\{0,1\}^{S}$ with the power set $2^{S}$. For $p\in(0,1)$, let $\mu_p^{S}$ be the probability measure on $2^{S}$ in which each element belongs to the sampled set independently with probability $p$. We use $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$. All graphs and probability spaces in the reduction are finite.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The game used in the reduction

A two-to-one game consists of a bipartite constraint graph with disjoint vertex sets $U,V$, nonempty label sets $X,Y$ with $\lvert X\rvert=2\lvert Y\rvert$, and, for each edge $e=(u,v)$, a map $\pi_e:X\to Y$ for which every fiber has cardinality two. Parallel constraints are allowed. The edges carry a rational probability distribution $\nu$. Its endpoint marginals are denoted by $\nu_U$ and $\nu_V$. A labeling consists of maps $\ell_U:U\to X$ and $\ell_V:V\to Y$; its value is $$\Pr_{e=(u,v)\sim\nu}
  [\,\pi_e(\ell_U(u))=\ell_V(v)\,].$$ The value of a game is the maximum of this expression over all labelings.

<a id="stmt-thm-games"></a>
<div id="thm:games" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (Two-to-one games with imperfect completeness). *For every constant $\theta\in(0,1/2)$, there are constant label sets $X,Y$ such that it is $\mathrm{NP}$-hard, by a deterministic polynomial-time reduction, to distinguish two-to-one games of the following kinds:*

1.  *there is a labeling of value at least $1-\theta$;*

2.  *every labeling has value at most $\theta$.*

*Every constraint is a total map $X\to Y$ with exactly two preimages of each label in $Y$. The label sets may depend on $\theta$.*

</div>

We invoke the completed theorem in this precise form. Dinur, Khot, Kindler, Minzer, and Safra prove the stated gap conditionally on their Grassmann agreement hypothesis (Dinur et al. 2025, Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[DKKMS2025](#ref-DKKMS2025)]. Their Hypothesis 3.6 follows from the Grassmann expansion theorem of Khot, Minzer, and Safra (Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2018, Theorem 1.12, ECCC revision 2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[KMS2018](#ref-KMS2018)] through the implications of Barak, Kothari, and Steurer (Barak, Kothari, and Steurer 2019, sec. 1.1 and Theorems 9–11) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BKS2019](#ref-BKS2019)]. The two-to-one definition and the common alphabets are as in (Dinur et al. 2025, Definitions 1.1–1.2 and Section 4.2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[DKKMS2025](#ref-DKKMS2025)]. In the explicit constraint construction, the smoothing parameter may be chosen rational within its permitted range (Dinur et al. 2025, sec. 4.2 and 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[DKKMS2025](#ref-DKKMS2025)]. The edge sampling process then specifies rational weights: all constant-length tuples and constant-dimensional subspaces can be enumerated, followed by the prescribed identifications. With the parameters fixed, this is a deterministic finite construction of polynomial size. Thus the use of a rational edge law does not introduce randomness into the reduction. We require neither perfect completeness nor any degree regularity of the game.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Junta approximation at fixed dyadic biases

For a Boolean function $f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ and $j\in S$, define <a id="eq:biased-influence"></a>$$\label{eq:biased-influence}
  I_{p,j}(f)
  =\Pr_{R\sim\mu_p^{S\setminus\{j\}}}
       [\,f(R)\ne f(R\cup\{j\})\,],
  \qquad
  I_p(f)=\sum_{j\in S} I_{p,j}(f).$$ This is also the probability that flipping coordinate $j$ changes the value of $f$, when the entire input has law $\mu_p^S$: the change event depends only on the other coordinates. A function is a *junta on $J\subseteq S$* if its value depends only on $R\cap J$. Constant functions are juntas on the empty set.

<a id="stmt-thm-friedgut"></a>
<div id="thm:friedgut" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Friedgut’s junta theorem). *For every $b\ge0$ and $\alpha>0$, there is a finite integer $J_{\mathrm{unif}}(b,\alpha)$ with the following property. For every finite $S$ and every Boolean function $f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ with $I_{1/2}(f)\le b$, there is a Boolean function $\widetilde f$, depending on at most $J_{\mathrm{unif}}(b,\alpha)$ coordinates, for which $$\Pr_{R\sim\mu_{1/2}^{S}}[\,f(R)\ne\widetilde f(R)\,]\le\alpha.$$ The bound is independent of $\lvert S\rvert$.*

</div>

This is the uniform version of Friedgut’s theorem (Friedgut 1998, Theorem 1.1 in the February 3, 1998 author manuscript) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Friedgut1998](#ref-Friedgut1998)]. Friedgut also proves a biased-product version (Friedgut 1998, Theorem 4.1 in the same manuscript) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Friedgut1998](#ref-Friedgut1998)]. We give an elementary reduction of the dyadic case to the uniform case, so that the dependence on the bias and independence of the game alphabet size are explicit.

<a id="stmt-cor-dyadic-junta"></a>
<div id="cor:dyadic-junta" class="corollary">

**Corollary 4** (Dyadic junta approximation). *Let $p=2^{-k}$, where $k\ge1$ is an integer. For every $b\ge0$ and $\alpha>0$, there is a finite integer $J(p,b,\alpha)$, independent of $\lvert S\rvert$, such that every Boolean function $f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ with $I_p(f)\le b$ has a Boolean junta approximant on at most $J(p,b,\alpha)$ coordinates with error at most $\alpha$ under $\mu_p^S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-dyadic-junta"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-dyadic-junta</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate for $S=\varnothing$, so assume otherwise. Let $(X_{j,a})_{j\in S,\,a\in[k]}$ be independent uniform bits and put $$Z_j=\bigwedge_{a=1}^k X_{j,a},
  \qquad
  F(X)=f(\{j\in S:Z_j=1\}).$$ The $Z_j$ are independent Bernoulli $p$ bits. Flipping $X_{j,a}$ changes $F$ precisely when all the other $k-1$ bits of its block are one and coordinate $j$ is pivotal for the values of the other blocks. These two events are independent. Therefore <a id="eq:and-block-influence"></a>$$\label{eq:and-block-influence}
  I_{1/2}(F)
   =k2^{-(k-1)}I_p(f)
   =2kpI_p(f)
   \le kb.$$ By Theorem <a href="#thm:friedgut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:friedgut">3</a>, there is a Boolean approximant $H$ to $F$ with error at most $\alpha$, using at most $J_{\mathrm{unif}}(kb,\alpha)$ of the individual bits $X_{j,a}$. Let $J\subseteq S$ be the set of blocks touched by these bits. Then $\lvert J\rvert\le J_{\mathrm{unif}}(kb,\alpha)$.

Reveal all bits in every block indexed by $J$. Since $H$ is measurable with respect to these revealed blocks, the Boolean predictor with smallest conditional error from the revealed blocks has total error at most that of $H$. More explicitly, this predictor outputs one when the conditional probability of $F=1$ is at least $1/2$, with any fixed convention at equality. The unrevealed blocks remain independent of the revealed blocks, and the dependence of $F$ on a revealed block is only through its AND value. Consequently $$\Pr[\,F=1\mid (X_{j,a})_{j\in J,\,a\in[k]}\,]
 =
 \Pr[\,f(\{j:Z_j=1\})=1\mid (Z_j)_{j\in J}\,].$$ The optimal predictor is therefore a Boolean function only of $(Z_j)_{j\in J}$. Regard it as a function $\widetilde f:2^S\to\{0,1\}$ on the original coordinates. The induced law of the original input is $\mu_p^S$, so $$\Pr_{R\sim\mu_p^S}[\,f(R)\ne\widetilde f(R)\,]\le\alpha.$$ Thus $J(p,b,\alpha)=J_{\mathrm{unif}}(kb,\alpha)$ is a valid choice, increased to one if desired. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 5*. The junta approximants need not be monotone. In the reduction, the original conditioned functions will be monotone, but the argument uses only their approximation errors and their coordinate supports. For any fixed finite collection of dyadic biases, the maximum of the bounds in Corollary <a href="#cor:dyadic-junta" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dyadic-junta">4</a> remains independent of the alphabet size.

</div>

<a id="sec:construction"></a>
### The weighted reduction

We first construct a digraph with positive rational vertex costs. Write $w_v$ for the cost of a vertex $v$ and $w(F)=\sum_{v\in F}w_v$ for the cost of a vertex set $F$. For each fixed integer $A\ge 1$, the construction will have a feedback vertex set of cost at most $4$ in the completeness case of Theorem <a href="#thm:games" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:games">2</a>, whereas every feedback vertex set will have cost greater than $D=10A$ in its soundness case. Section <a href="#sec:unweighting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:unweighting">8</a> converts this gap into an unweighted one.

<a id="sec:parameters"></a>
#### Choice of parameters

Fix an integer $A\ge 1$ and put $D=10A$. The constants have the following roles. There will be $M$ bias scales and at most $T$ prefix slots. The full-prefix and comparison tests have total costs $H$ and $K$. The junta size is bounded by $J$, independently of the game alphabets; $\theta$ is the required game accuracy. Finally $L$ and $N$ specify finite ordered rank sets, and $\psi:[L]\hookrightarrow[N]$ is an increasing random embedding. Its purpose is to make the order comparisons insensitive to their precise rank levels, as proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-spreading" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-spreading">13</a>. Choose the constants in the following order.

1.  Set $$M=128(D+1),\qquad
        p_i=2^{-(i+1)},\qquad q_i=2p_i\quad (i\in[M]),
        \qquad \gamma=\frac{p_M}{1000}.$$

<a id="eq:prefix-length-choice"></a>
2.  Set $H=8(D+1)$. Choose an integer $T\ge 1$ large enough that $$HM\exp\left(-\frac{p_MT}{8M}\right)\le 1,
        \label{eq:prefix-length-choice}$$ and an integer <a id="eq:comparison-mass-choice"></a>$$K\ge \frac{M2^T D}{\gamma}.
        \label{eq:comparison-mass-choice}$$

<a id="eq:game-accuracy-choice"></a>
3.  Put $B_0=(D+1)/\gamma$. By Corollary <a href="#cor:dyadic-junta" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:dyadic-junta">4</a>, there is an integer $J\ge 1$ that bounds the size of a junta approximant with error at most $\gamma$ for every function of total influence at most $B_0$ at any of the biases $p_i,q_i$, $i\in[M]$. Choose a positive rational $\theta$ small enough that Theorem <a href="#thm:games" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:games">2</a> applies and $$K\theta\le 1,\qquad J^2\theta\le\gamma.
        \label{eq:game-accuracy-choice}$$ Apply that theorem with this fixed $\theta$, and let $X,Y$ be its alphabets, with $m=\lvert X\rvert=2\lvert Y\rvert$.

4.  Put $$k_{\max}=2^Tm,\qquad L=10k_{\max}+30,
        \qquad \rho=\min\{\gamma,1/m\}.$$ Choose a positive rational $\eta$ satisfying <a id="eq:rank-accuracy-choice"></a>$$3\eta L^3 2^{m^T}\le\rho.
        \label{eq:rank-accuracy-choice}$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-spreading" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-spreading">13</a> to $L,\eta$, obtaining a finite $N$ and a rational probability distribution on increasing maps $\psi:[L]\hookrightarrow[N]$. Fix this distribution for the construction.

All these quantities depend only on $A$. In particular, the junta bound $J$ is fixed *before* the alphabet size $m$ is determined. Its existence does not require an alphabet bound. The later choices of $\rho,\eta,N$ therefore introduce no dependence back into the choice of $\theta$.

Let the input game have bipartition $U,V$, edge distribution $\nu$, and endpoint marginals $\nu_U,\nu_V$. For an edge $e=(u,v)$, write $\pi_e:X\to Y$ for its two-to-one projection. All probability distributions below are finite and rational. A sampling description specifies a separate vertex for each outcome of positive probability, with the indicated probability as a factor in its cost. The reduction enumerates these outcomes; it does not sample a random output graph.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Rank vertices and their identifications

<a id="stmt-def-rank-graph"></a>
<div id="def:rank-graph" class="definition">

<a id="eq:tuple-domain-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:prefix-identification"></a>
**Definition 6** (Rank graph). For $w\in U\cup V$, let $\Lambda_w=X$ if $w\in U$ and $\Lambda_w=Y$ if $w\in V$, and put $m_w=\lvert\Lambda_w\rvert$. The allowed vertex tuples are $$\bigcup_{t=0}^{T}U^t
  \quad\cup\quad
  \bigcup_{t=0}^{T-1}(U^t\times V).$$ Tuples are ordered sequences and may contain repeated vertices. For an allowed tuple $\mathbf d=(w_1,\ldots,w_t)$, let $\Lambda_{\mathbf d}=\prod_{j=1}^t\Lambda_{w_j}$; the product for the empty tuple is a singleton. In every case $$\lvert\Lambda_{\mathbf d}\rvert\le m^T.
  \label{eq:tuple-domain-bound}$$ For every function $x:\Lambda_{\mathbf d}\to[N]$, introduce a formal rank vertex $b_{\mathbf d}(x)$. Within each tuple, introduce an arc $b_{\mathbf d}(x)\to b_{\mathbf d}(y)$ whenever $$x(\lambda)<y(\lambda)
  \qquad\text{for every }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbf d}.$$ Whenever $\mathbf s w$ is an allowed extension of $\mathbf s$, identify $$b_{\mathbf s}(x)
  \equiv b_{\mathbf s w}(x\circ\operatorname{proj}),
  \label{eq:prefix-identification}$$ where $\operatorname{proj}:\Lambda_{\mathbf s w}\to\Lambda_{\mathbf s}$ drops the last coordinate. Take the equivalence relation generated by all these identifications and keep the induced arcs between its classes. Each resulting rank vertex has cost $D+1$, independently of the number of its formal representatives. We use the same notation for a formal vertex and its equivalence class. The vertices represented by the constant profiles with value $j$ are denoted by $o_j$, for $j\in[N]$.

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-evaluation-invariant"></a>
<div id="lem:evaluation-invariant" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Evaluation invariant). *Every global game labeling $\ell$ gives a well-defined real value on the rank vertices by $$b_{\mathbf d}(x)\longmapsto x(\ell(\mathbf d)),$$ where $\ell(\mathbf d)$ is the tuple of labels supplied by $\ell$. Every rank arc strictly increases this value. Consequently, the rank graph has no self-loops, and $o_1,\ldots,o_N$ are distinct vertices with an arc $o_j\to o_{j'}$ whenever $j<j'$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-evaluation-invariant"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-evaluation-invariant</summary>

*Proof.* For every generating identification in Equation <a href="#eq:prefix-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-identification">[eq:prefix-identification]</a>, $$(x\circ\operatorname{proj})(\ell(\mathbf s),\ell(w))
  =x(\ell(\mathbf s)).$$ Thus evaluation is constant on each equivalence class. This remains true when vertices repeat in a tuple: their repeated coordinates are evaluated at the same global label. A rank arc is strictly increasing at every tuple of labels, and hence at the particular tuple supplied by $\ell$. Its two endpoints cannot become the same equivalence class. Finally, constant profiles all identify with their representatives at the empty tuple, and their evaluated values are the distinct integers $1,\ldots,N$. ◻

</details>

This division into protected rank vertices and deletable tests is related to the bit-vertex/test-vertex architecture of (Svensson 2013, sec. 3.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Svensson2013](#ref-Svensson2013)]; here the rank profiles and prefix identifications enforce the consistency needed below. All other vertices will be test vertices, distinct from the rank vertices and from one another. Their arcs will be incident only to rank vertices. For $E\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf d}$ and $1\le a<h\le L$, write $$b_{\mathbf d}^{\psi}(E;a,h)=b_{\mathbf d}(x),\qquad
  x(\lambda)=
  \begin{cases}
    \psi(a),&\lambda\in E,\\
    \psi(h),&\lambda\notin E.
  \end{cases}$$

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Wildcard vertices and prefix data

<a id="stmt-def-wildcard"></a>
<div id="def:wildcard" class="definition">

<a id="eq:wildcard-incoming"></a>
<a id="eq:wildcard-outgoing"></a>
**Definition 8** (Wildcard vertex). Fix an allowed tuple $\mathbf d$, an increasing map $\psi$, an ordered partition $C_1,\ldots,C_k$ of $\Lambda_{\mathbf d}$ with $1\le k\le k_{\max}$, and a set $Z\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf d}$, called the star set. Empty cells in the partition are permitted. Put $z_\ell=\psi(10\ell+10)$ for $\ell\in[k]$. The corresponding wildcard vertex has an incoming arc from every rank vertex $b_{\mathbf d}(x)$ for which $$x(\lambda)<z_\ell
  \quad\text{for all }\ell\in[k]
  \text{ and }\lambda\in C_\ell\setminus Z,
  \label{eq:wildcard-incoming}$$ and an outgoing arc to every $b_{\mathbf d}(x)$ satisfying the strict reverse inequalities $$x(\lambda)>z_\ell
  \quad\text{for all }\ell\in[k]
  \text{ and }\lambda\in C_\ell\setminus Z.
  \label{eq:wildcard-outgoing}$$ There are no restrictions on the values of $x$ on $Z$.

</div>

The wildcard inequalities have a direct interpretation under any global labeling. If its evaluated tuple lies in $C_r\setminus Z$, the threshold $z_r$ lies strictly between the evaluations of every incoming and every outgoing rank vertex. Assigning that threshold to the test therefore extends the increasing evaluation along its incident arcs. At starred tuples, the arc conditions impose no restriction on these evaluated values; the completeness argument will delete the corresponding test.

<a id="stmt-def-prefix-data"></a>
<div id="def:prefix-data" class="definition">

**Definition 9** (Prefix data). For $t\in\{0,\ldots,T\}$, draw $\mathbf s=(u_1,\ldots,u_t)$ independently from $\nu_U$ in each slot. Independently draw $i_1,\ldots,i_t$ uniformly from $[M]$. Conditional on these indices, draw $P_j\subseteq X$ using independent Bernoulli $p_{i_j}$ membership bits, independently for different slots $j$. Partition $\Lambda_{\mathbf s}$ by the membership patterns $$\bigl(\mathbf 1\{\lambda_j\in P_j\}\bigr)_{j=1}^t,
  \qquad \lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbf s}.$$ Order the $2^t$ cells lexicographically with $1$ before $0$, keeping cells that happen to be empty, and denote them by $C_1,\ldots,C_{2^t}$. Put $$C_{<r}=\bigcup_{\ell<r}C_\ell,
  \qquad C_{\le r}=\bigcup_{\ell\le r}C_\ell.$$ At $t=0$ this is the one-cell partition of the singleton product domain.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The three test families

<a id="stmt-item-comparison-test"></a>
<div id="item:comparison-test" class="definition">

**Definition 10** (Test families). <span id="def:tests" label="def:tests"></span> Add the following families of test vertices to the rank graph. In each family, independently draw $\psi$ from the fixed rank distribution. Each outcome is a separate vertex with cost equal to its probability multiplied by the stated total mass. Distinct outcomes remain separate even if they produce the same incident arcs.

1.  **Full-prefix tests, of total mass $H$.** <span id="item:full-prefix-test" label="item:full-prefix-test"></span> Draw prefix data at length $T$ and use the wildcard vertex in tuple $\mathbf s$ with those $2^T$ cells. Its star set is <a id="eq:full-prefix-stars"></a>$$Z=\left\{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbf s}:
          \text{there exists }i\in[M]\text{ such that }
          \#\{j:i_j=i,\ \lambda_j\in P_j\}
          <\frac{p_iT}{2M}\right\}.
        \label{eq:full-prefix-stars}$$

2.  **Single-label tests, with one family on each side.** <span id="item:single-label-test" label="item:single-label-test"></span> The big-side family has total mass $\lvert X\rvert$, and the small-side family has total mass $\lvert Y\rvert$. Choose $t$ uniformly from $\{0,\ldots,T-1\}$ and draw prefix data at length $t$. Independently draw $w$ from the endpoint marginal on the chosen side, a uniformly random label $j_0\in\Lambda_w$, and a uniformly random permutation $(\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(m_w))$ of $\Lambda_w$. Use a wildcard vertex in tuple $\mathbf s w$. Its cells, in order, are $$C_\ell\times\{\sigma(a)\},
